{\bm{0}}_{m}\end{pmatrix}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d+m)\times m}.

Then, the joint covariance
{\Omega^{\star}}
has support in the subspace
\text{ker}({G^{\star}})\otimes\mathbb{R}^{m}
and vanishes in the subspace
\text{span}({G^{\star}}^{T})\otimes{\bm{0}}_{m}
.

5.3
An estimator of the covariance matrix

We analyze a plug-in covariance matrix estimator. The sketch-related quantities in (
5.9
),
C^{\star}=\mathbb{E}[\widetilde{C}^{\star}]
and
P^{\star}=\mathbb{E}[(I+\widetilde{C}^{\star}){\Omega^{\star}}(I+\widetilde{C}^{\star})^{T}]
, can be estimated by computing (note
\widetilde{C}^{\star}
is defined in (
5.4
) and
\Omega_{t}
is defined in (
5.15
))

\displaystyle\widetilde{C}_{t}\coloneqq-\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}\left(I-K_{t}S_{t,j}(S_{t,j}^{T}K_{t}^{2}S_{t,j})^{\dagger}S_{t,j}^{T}K_{t}\right),

\displaystyle\widehat{C}_{t}\coloneqq\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}\widetilde{C}_{i},\quad\quad\text{and}\quad\quad\widehat{P}_{t}\coloneqq\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}(I+\widetilde{C}_{i})\Omega_{i}(I+\widetilde{C}_{i})^{T}.

Since solving Lyapunov equation requires additional effort and
\widehat{P}_{t}
requires matrix
inverse
even if we do not perform inference at the current step, in what follows, we are motivated by Corollary
5.7
and simply neglect the sketch-related quantities in (
5.9
) by estimating (
5.12
) instead. We demonstrate that such negligence only leads to an
O(\rho^{\tau})
error term, which is generally small even for a moderate
\tau
. Specifically, our estimator of
\Xi^{\star}
is defined as:

\Omega_{t}=K_{t}^{-1}\begin{pmatrix}{sample\_cov}(\{{\bar{g}}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{t-1})&{\bm{0}}\\
{\bm{0}}&{\bm{0}}\end{pmatrix}K_{t}^{-1}\quad\text{ and }\quad\Xi_{t}=\frac{\Omega_{t}}{2+\beta/\widetilde{\beta}},

(5.15)

where
{sample\_cov}(\{{\bar{g}}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{t-1})=\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}{\bar{g}}_{i}{\bar{g}}_{i}^{T}-\left(\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}{\bar{g}}_{i}\right)\left(\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}{\bar{g}}_{i}\right)^{T}
is the sample
covariance
.

Theorem 5.10
.

Consider (
5.15
) under the conditions of Theorem
5.6
with (
4.2c
). For any
\upsilon>0
,

\|\Xi_{t}-\Xi^{\star}\|=O\big(\sqrt{\beta_{t}\log(1/\beta_{t})}\;\big)+o\big(\sqrt{(\log t)^{1+\upsilon}/t}\big)+O(\rho^{\tau})\quad\text{a.s.}

(5.16)

Furthermore, for any
\bm{w}=(\bm{w}_{{\bm{x}}},\bm{w}_{{\bm{\lambda}}})\in\mathbb{R}^{d+m}
such that
\bm{w}^{T}\Xi_{t}\bm{w}\neq 0
,

\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left|P\left(\frac{\sqrt{1/{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}}\cdot\bm{w}^{T}({\bm{x}}_{t}-{\bm{x}}^{\star},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}-{\bm{\lambda}}^{\star})}{\sqrt{\bm{w}^{T}\Xi_{t}\bm{w}}}\leq z\right)-P\left({\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\leq z\right)\right|\\
=o(\beta_{t}^{1/6}\log(1/\beta_{t}))+O(\chi_{t}^{q}/\beta_{t}^{q+0.5})+O(\rho^{\tau}),

(5.17)

where
q\in(0,1]
is defined in Theorem
5.6
.

The second term
o\big(\sqrt{(\log t)^{1+\upsilon}/t}\big)
can be absorbed into the first term
O\big(\sqrt{\beta_{t}\log(1/\beta_{t})}\;\big)
if
\beta>-1
, e.g.,
\beta_{t}=1/t^{\omega}
for
\omega\in(0,1)
. We require condition (
4.2c
) for the estimation of the limiting covariance, which ensures the convergence of the sample covariance of
\{{\bar{g}}_{i}\}_{i=0}^{t-1}
. The bounded
4
-th moment of
{\bar{g}}_{t}
is also standard in the literature, as required for analyzing different covariance estimators for SGD methods
(
Chen et al. 2020
;
Zhu et al. 2021
)
.

Remark 5.11

(Discussion on control sequences
\{\beta_{t},\chi_{t}=\eta_{t}-\beta_{t}\}
)
.

We note that the normality in (
5.8
) utilizes the adaptive stepsize
{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}
, which nevertheless is controlled by the sequences
\{\beta_{t},\chi_{t}\}
. We discuss their conditions in this remark.

The global convergence requires (
4.4
) (Theorem
4.8
); the local convergence requires (
5.7
) (Theorem
5.5
); and the inference additionally requires
\chi<1.5\beta
(Theorem
5.6
). In fact, by Raabe’s test, (
4.4
) also relates to the quantities
\beta
and
\chi
: (
4.4
) holds if
-1\leq\beta<-0.5
and
\chi<-1
. We now specialize
\beta_{t}
and
\chi_{t}
to be polynomial in
t
, and demonstrate that the conditions can be easily satisfied.

Lemma 5.12
.

Suppose
\beta_{t}=c_{1}/t^{c_{2}}
and
\chi_{t}=\beta_{t}^{c_{3}}
. Then,

(a):

(
4.4
) holds
if

c_{1}>0
,
c_{2}\in(0.5,1]
,
c_{3}>\frac{1}{c_{2}}

\Longrightarrow
global convergence.

(b):

(
4.4
) and (
5.7
) hold
if
additionally
c_{1}>\frac{1}{1.5(1-\rho^{\tau})}
when
c_{2}=1

\Longrightarrow
local convergence.

(c):

(
4.4
) and (
5.7
) hold with
\chi<1.5\beta

if
additionally
c_{3}>1.5\vee\frac{1}{c_{2}}

\Longrightarrow
asymptotic inference.

The proof of the above lemma is immediate by noting that
\beta=-c_{2}
,
\chi=-c_{2}c_{3}
, and
\widetilde{\beta}=c_{1}
if
c_{2}=1
and
\infty
if
c_{2}<1
. Thus, we omit it.

6
Numerical Experiments

We provide experimental results in this section. We apply
AI-StoSQP
to both
benchmark
constrained nonlinear optimization problems in CUTEst set
(
Gould et al. 2014
)
and to linearly/ nonlinearly constrained regression problems. For regression problems, we explore both squared loss and logistic loss. For conciseness, some of results are deferred to Appendix
F
.

6.1
Benchmark constrained problems

The CUTEst test set collects a number of nonlinear optimization problems with and without constraints. We implement eight equality-constrained problems:
MARATOS
,
ORTHREGB
,
HS7
,
HS48
,
HS78
,
BT9
,
GENHS28
,
HS39
. The solution of each problem is solved by IPOPT solver
(
Wächter and Biegler 2006
)
with the initialization specified by the CUTEst package. Note that the benchmark problems may not have unique solutions; however, we observed that by
initializing
at the same point, our StoSQP method consistently converges to the same
solution
as IPOPT, which is also a widely used (deterministic) SQP-based solver.

For our method, we perform
10^{5}
iterations and, at each step, we perform
\tau=40
randomized Kaczmarz steps to approximately solve QPs. Given the iterate
{\bm{x}}_{t}
, we generate
{\bar{g}}_{t}\sim{\mathcal{N}}(\nabla f_{t},\sigma^{2}(I+{\bm{1}}{\bm{1}}^{T}))
, where
{\bm{1}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}
is an all-one vector. We also generate the
(i,j)
and
(j,i)
entries of
{\bar{H}}_{t}
from
{\mathcal{N}}((\nabla^{2}f_{t})_{i,j},\sigma^{2})
. We vary
\sigma^{2}\in\{10^{-4},10^{-2},10^{-1},1\}
and let
\beta_{t}=1/t^{0.501}
(power slightly larger than
0.5
) and
\chi_{t}=\beta_{t}^{2}
. We randomly choose
{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}\sim\text{Uniform}([\beta_{t},\eta_{t}])
with
\eta_{t}=\beta_{t}+\chi_{t}
.
For each problem, we aim to perform inference for each individual
variable

\{{\bm{x}}^{\star}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
by setting the nominal coverage probability to 95%. Note from Remark
5.9
that
\Omega_{{\bm{x}},{\bm{x}}}^{\star}
may vanish along the direction in the normal space
\text{span}({G^{\star}}^{T})
. Thus, we consider inferring only those
{\bm{x}}^{\star}_{i}
such that the canonical basis
\bm{e}_{i}\notin\text{span}({G^{\star}}^{T})
.
The confidence intervals are constructed by estimating the limiting covariance using Theorem
5.10
. The performance of the method is measured by the mean absolute error (MAE)
\|{\bm{x}}_{t}-{\bm{x}}^{\star}\|
, the average coverage rate (Avg Cov) of the confidence intervals among individuals
{\bm{x}}^{\star}_{i}
, the average length (Avg Len) of the confidence intervals, and the computational flops per iteration. We repeat the experiments 200 times when computing the coverage rate.

Table 1:
Results of eight benchmark CUTEst problems. We measure performance using the mean absolute error (MAE), the average coverage rate (Ave Cov) and the average length (Ave Len) of the confidence intervals, and the computational flops per iteration. Standard errors are reported in the bracket for MAE and Ave Len. We do not report standard errors for Ave Cov as they are meaningless for a 0-1 indicator vector; the standard error is given by
\sqrt{r(1-r)}
with
r
being the coverage rate. The flops/iter is uniform over different noise level
\sigma^{2}
and different runs (i.e., the standard error is 0).

The results are summarized in Table
1
. From Table
1
, we have the following observations.

(a)

In terms of MAE, our method achieves reasonably small MAE values across all problems. As the noise level
\sigma^{2}
for the objective gradient and Hessian estimates gradually increases, the MAE also increases. This aligns with our intuition, as noisier
estimates
of the
objective
quantities will require more samples, i.e., longer iterations, to ensure the accuracy of the
estimate

{\bm{x}}_{t}
.

(b)

In terms of coverage rate, we observe for the majority of cases that our constructed confidence intervals cover the true solution with probability of at least
95\%
, and the coverage rate is robust to the sampling variance
\sigma^{2}
.
There are scenarios, such as
HS78
and
ORTHREGB
(
\sigma^{2}=10^{-4}
), where our confidence intervals may exhibit over- or under-coverage. This phenomenon can be attributed to two factors: (i) our limiting covariance estimate has an
O(\rho^{\tau})
bias due to sketching techniques, which may either inflate or deflate the estimated variance for each individual variable, and (ii) we run only a limited number of steps (i.e., the standardized sequence may have not yet reached a stationary stage), given the problem’s scale and the challenges inherent in the online inference task.
Nevertheless, our observation suggests that neglecting the sketching randomness in the estimation of the limiting covariance does not obviously deteriorate the coverage rate.
However
,
using
(sparse)
sketching

vectors
to solve QPs as in (
3.4
) is computationally more efficient than exact second-order methods.

(c)

In terms of confidence intervals’ length, we see that the average length gradually increases as
\sigma^{2}
increases. When
\sigma^{2}
increases from
10^{-4}
to
1
, the length increases from
10^{-4}
to
10^{-2}
. This outcome is expected, as the asymptotic covariance
\Xi^{\star}
depends on
\text{cov}(\nabla f({\bm{x}}^{\star};\xi))
in (
5.9
).

(d)

In terms of computational flops per iteration, it is uniform over different
\sigma^{2}
and independent runs. This quantity basically reflects the problem scale.

We testify the almost sure convergence rate of our method (Theorem
5.5
) in Appendix
F.1
.

6.2
Constrained regression problems

We implement our method on constrained regression problems, considering both linear regression and logistic regression (see Example
2.1
). We also allow for either
linear

constraints

A{\bm{x}}={\bm{d}}
or nonlinear constraints
\|{\bm{x}}\|^{2}=b
. Therefore, there are four cases in total. We
compare
our online inference method with offline constrained
M
-estimation. For fair comparisons, we solve
M
-estimation problems, which are constrained finite-sum problems, using
\ell_{1}
-penalized SQP methods with backtracking line search to select proper stepsizes
(
Nocedal and Wright 2006
)
. While offline
M
-estimators enjoy asymptotic minimax optimal performance with the least
covariance
(as introduced in (
1.3
)), the proposed online StoSQP method is promising due to its lower per-iteration computational complexity. (Our method also achieves optimal performance
under
appropriate setups; cf. Corollary
5.7
.)

To be specific, for each case (regression model + constraint type), we vary the
parameter
dimension
d\hskip-1.0pt\in\hskip-1.0pt\{5,20,40,60\}
, and the true solution
{\bm{x}}^{\star}
is linearly spaced between 0 and 1. For each
d
, our method randomly samples a covariate
\xi_{{\bm{a}}}\sim\mathcal{N}({\bm{0}},5I+\Sigma_{a})
at each step, with three different choices of
\Sigma_{a}
(also considered in
Chen et al. 2020
). (i) Identity:
\Sigma_{a}=I
. (ii) Toeplitz:
[\Sigma_{a}]_{i,j}=r^{|i-j|}
. (iii) Equi-correlation:
[\Sigma_{a}]_{i,j}=r
for
i\neq j
and
[\Sigma_{a}]_{i,i}=1
. For
linear
constraints, we let
A\in\mathbb{R}^{m\times d}
with
m=\lceil\sqrt{d}\rceil
and entries being independently generated from standard normal distribution. For logistic models, we regularize the loss by a quadratic penalty with unit parameter. Following Section
6.1
, we perform inference for each individual
variable

\{{\bm{x}}^{\star}_{i}\}_{i=1}^{d}
by setting the nominal coverage probability to 95%.
We also follow Section
6.1
to implement our method, including the number of iterations, the setup of the
stepsize
, and the number of sketching steps.
In contrast to online methods, the offline
M
-estimation method generates all
10^{5}
samples at once and uses those fixed samples to compute the estimator. The covariance matrix is also estimated by a plug-in estimator. We
report
the
results
only for
r=0.5
for Toeplitz and
r=0.2
for Equi-correlation. The comprehensive comparisons between inexact and exact methods with varying
d,r
and
\tau
are reported in Section
F.2
.

Table 3:
Comparison results of online StoSQP and offline
M
-estimation for constrained regression problems (logistic models). For each cell, the top row shows the result of the
M
-estimator, while the bottom row shows the result of StoSQP.

We summarize the comparison results in Tables
2
and
3
, including the mean absolute error (MAE)
\|{\bm{x}}_{t}-{\bm{x}}^{\star}\|
, the average coverage rate (Avg Cov) of the confidence intervals among individuals
{\bm{x}}^{\star}_{i}
, the average length (Avg Len) of the confidence intervals, and the computational flops per iteration. From Tables
2
and
3
, we summarize the following observations.

(a)

In terms of MAE and Ave Len, offline constrained
M
-estimators achieve results that are
an order of magnitude smaller
than those of online StoSQP. This gap aligns with our analysis from two perspectives. First,
M
-estimators exhibit
\sqrt{n}
-consistency and optimal
asymptotic
covariance, while StoSQP estimators exhibit only
\sqrt{1/{\bar{\alpha}}_{n}}
-consistency, where
{\bar{\alpha}}_{n}
denotes the stepsize (in this case,
\sqrt{n^{0.501}}
). Clearly, as long as
{\bar{\alpha}}_{n}\not\asymp 1/n
, the covariance of StoSQP estimators is not comparable to that of
M
-estimators when the estimators are scaled by the same scalar. Second, the randomization of the sketching solver within StoSQP introduces additional uncertainty to the estimators, further enlarging the asymptotic covariance (cf. Corollary
5.7
), although this enlargement is controlled by the precision of the sketching solver and decreases exponentially with the number of sketching steps.

(b)

In terms of Ave Cov, the proposed online StoSQP method achieves promising coverage rates that are very close to 95% for both linear and logistic models as well as for both linear and nonlinear constraints, matching the performance of offline
M
-estimators. The only
potential
exception occurs when
d=5
, where StoSQP may exhibit undercoverage (around 90%) for linear and logistic models. Upon closer examination of these scenarios, we find that the
condition
number of the Lagrangian Hessian of these problems exceeds
d^{3}=125
, indicating that these problems are ill-conditioned and difficult to solve, particularly when using a sketching solver that seems overkill (see (
4.3
)). Furthermore, SGD-based estimators are also observed to
exhibit
undercoverage for various problems due to significant challenges of online inference tasks
(
Zhu et al. 2021
)
. To our knowledge, StoSQP is the first method capable of conducting online statistical inference for constrained model parameters. While projection-based estimators
(
Duchi and Ruan 2021
;
Davis et al. 2024
)
may demonstrate similar asymptotic normality, estimating their limiting covariance remains unclear.

(c)

In terms of computational flops per iteration, offline
M
-estimation involves processing the full batch of samples, resulting in significant computational and memory costs. In
contrast
, our online method processes a single sample, requiring significantly fewer computations. Additionally, the inexact sketching solver for solving Newton systems further reduces the dominant computational cost of the
proposed second-order method.
Overall
, the
reduced

computational
flops per iteration are a major advantage of our online StoSQP method over offline methods.

Further discussions of exact and inexact StoSQP methods are provided in Section
F.2
.

7
Conclusion and Future Work

We performed statistical inference of nonlinearly constrained stochastic optimization problems using a fully online second-order method called Stochastic Sequential Quadratic Programming (StoSQP). In each iteration, the scheme selects a proper adaptive stepsize and inexactly solves the Newton system (a quadratic program) by a randomized
sketching
solver.
Consequently
, the considered method is more adaptive and computationally efficient than existing exact second-order methods.
For this method, we established an almost sure convergence rate and iteration complexity, and proved the asymptotic normality property for the last iterate. We
observed
that although the limiting covariance is worse than the minimax optimum achieved by constrained
M
-estimators and online projection-based estimators, the gap decays exponentially fast in terms of the number of iterations employed for the sketching solver (e.g., the
covariance
matches the optimum if using exact QP solvers). Additionally, we analyzed a plug-in covariance
matrix
estimator. Our analysis precisely quantified the uncertainty of the stochastic process
generated
by StoSQP methods, which encompasses the randomness of sampling as well as the computation (sketching and stepsize). The randomness of computation is particularly important for second-order methods to be efficient in practice. With our results, one can apply
AI-StoSQP
to perform online inference for constrained estimation problems.

As for future directions, it is of interest to provide a non-asymptotic analysis for StoSQP methods. Such a result would complement our analysis by bounding the distance between the distribution of
({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t})
and the normal distribution for any given
t
.
Furthermore, recent literature on online inference has explored different test statistics, whose asymptotic distributions rely on the application of the Functional Central Limit Theorem
(
Lee et al. 2022
;
Luo et al. 2022
;
Li et al. 2023
;
Roy and Balasubramanian 2023
;
Chen et al. 2024
)
.
Establishing
a functional CLT for second-order methods and studying the limiting distribution of random scaling estimators is also an interesting research direction.
Finally, incorporating nonlinear inequality constraints into the problems and developing second-order methods without projections also deserves further study in future work.

acknowledgments-disclosure-of-funding.
SN would like to acknowledge Xinchen Du, Yuefeng Han, and Wanrong Zhu for the helpful discussions of the work.
MWM would like to acknowledge the NSF, ONR, and a J. P. Morgan Chase Faculty Research Award for providing partial support of this work. This work was also supported by the U.S. Department of Energy, Office of Science, Office of Advanced Scientific Computing Research, Scientific Discovery through Advanced Computing (SciDAC) program, under Contract Number DE-AC02-05CH11231 at Lawrence Berkeley National Laboratory.

Appendix A
An Example of Stepsize Selection Scheme

We consider selecting a stepsize to decrease the penalized objective

\phi_{\nu}({\bm{x}};\xi)\coloneqq\nu F({\bm{x}};\xi)+\|c({\bm{x}})\|.

We note that decreasing the objective
F({\bm{x}};\xi)
only is not reasonable for constrained problems, since we may violate constraints arbitrarily. The local linear approximation of
\phi_{\nu}({\bm{x}};\xi)
at
({\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t})
along the direction
{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t}
is

\phi^{\text{loc}}_{\nu}({\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t},{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t})\coloneqq\nu(F({\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t})+{\bar{g}}_{t}^{T}{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t})+\|c_{t}+G_{t}{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t}\|.

We can further define the local model reduction, a negative quantity for sufficiently small
\nu>0
and approximation error, as

\displaystyle\Delta\phi_{\nu}^{\text{loc}}({\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t},{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t})

\displaystyle\coloneqq\phi^{\text{loc}}_{\nu}({\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t},{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t})-\phi^{\text{loc}}_{\nu}({\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t},{\bm{0}})

\displaystyle=\nu{\bar{g}}_{t}^{T}{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t}+\|c_{t}+G_{t}{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t}\|-\|c_{t}\|.

(A.1)

For a given scalar
\kappa_{t}\in(0,1)
, we select
{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}
such that
\phi_{\nu}({\bm{x}}_{t}+{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t})
decreases
\phi_{\nu}({\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t})
by at least a factor of
\kappa_{t}{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}
of the local model reduction
\Delta\phi_{\nu}^{\text{loc}}
(so called the Armijo condition). Specifically, we require

\phi_{\nu}({\bm{x}}_{t}+{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t})\leq\phi_{\nu}({\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t})+\kappa_{t}{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}\cdot\Delta\phi_{\nu}^{\text{loc}}({\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t},{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t}).

(A.2)

To satisfy (
A.2
), we suppose
\nabla F({\bm{x}};\xi_{t})
and
G({\bm{x}})
are local Lipschitz continuous around
{\bm{x}}_{t}
, and suppose
{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}\leq 1
. Then, there exists a constant
\Upsilon_{\nu,t}>0
, such that

\displaystyle\phi_{\nu}({\bm{x}}_{t}+{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t})=\nu F({\bm{x}}_{t}+{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}};\xi_{t})+\|c({\bm{x}}_{t}+{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}})\|

\displaystyle\leq\phi_{\nu}({\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t})+\nu{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}{\bar{g}}_{t}^{T}{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t}+\|c_{t}+{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}G_{t}{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t}\|-\|c_{t}\|+\Upsilon_{\nu,t}{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}^{2}\|{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}\;\;(\text{since }\nabla F,G\text{ are Lip})

\displaystyle\leq\phi_{\nu}({\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t})+\nu{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}{\bar{g}}_{t}^{T}{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t}+{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}\|c_{t}+G_{t}{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t}\|-{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}\|c_{t}\|+\Upsilon_{\nu,t}{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}^{2}\|{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}\;\;(\text{since }{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}\leq 1)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:reduction}}}}{{=}}\;\phi_{\nu}({\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t})+{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}\cdot\Delta\phi_{\nu}^{\text{loc}}({\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t},{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t})+\Upsilon_{\nu,t}{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}^{2}\|{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}.

Therefore, (
A.2
) is satisfied as long as

{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}\leq\frac{(\kappa_{t}-1)\cdot\Delta\phi_{\nu}^{\text{loc}}({\bm{x}}_{t};\xi_{t},{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t})}{\Upsilon_{\nu,t}\|{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}}\wedge 1\eqqcolon{\bar{\alpha}}_{t,thres}.

(A.3)

The Lipschitz constant
\Upsilon_{\nu,t}
can be estimated around
{\bm{x}}_{t}

(
Curtis and Robinson 2018
)
or simply prespecified as a large constant. The condition (
A.3
) leads us to propose
{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}\coloneqq\text{Proj}_{[\beta_{t},\eta_{t}]}({\bar{\alpha}}_{t,thres})
. See
Berahas et al. 2021
;
Berahas et al. 2023
;
Curtis et al. 2021
for detailed random projections and
Hong et al. 2023
for adaptive selection of parameters of line search functions (e.g.
\nu
).

Appendix B
Preparation Lemmas

Lemma B.1
.

Suppose
\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i}
is a positive sequence that satisfies
\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}i(1-\varphi_{i-1}/\varphi_{i})=\varphi
. Then, for any
p\geq 0
, we have
\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}i\left(1-\varphi_{i-1}^{p}/\varphi_{i}^{p}\right)=p\cdot\varphi
.

Lemma B.2
.

Let
\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i}
be a positive sequence. If
\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}i(1-\varphi_{i-1}/\varphi_{i})=\varphi<0
, then
\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}\varphi_{i}=0
.

Lemma B.3
.

Let
\{\phi_{i}\}_{i}
,
\{\varphi_{i}\}_{i}
,
\{\sigma_{i}\}_{i}
be three positive sequences. Suppose

\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}i\left(1-\phi_{i-1}/\phi_{i}\right)=\phi<0,\quad\quad\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}\varphi_{i}=0,\quad\quad\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}i\varphi_{i}=\widetilde{\varphi}

(B.1)

for a constant
\phi
and a (possibly infinite) constant
\widetilde{\varphi}\in(0,\infty]
. For any
l\geq 1
, if we further have
\sum_{k=1}^{l}\sigma_{k}+p\phi/\widetilde{\varphi}>0
for some constant
p\in(0,1]
, then the following results hold as
t\rightarrow\infty

(a):

When
p=1
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\phi_{t}}\sum_{i=0}^{t}\prod_{j=i+1}^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})\varphi_{i}\phi_{i}\longrightarrow\frac{1}{\sum_{k=1}^{l}\sigma_{k}+\phi/\widetilde{\varphi}},

(B.2)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\phi_{t}}\left\{\sum_{i=0}^{t}\prod_{j=i+1}^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})\varphi_{i}\phi_{i}a_{i}+b\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})\right\}\longrightarrow 0,

where the second result holds for any constant
b
and sequence
\{a_{t}\}_{t}
such that
a_{t}\rightarrow 0
.

(b):

When
p\in(0,1)
,

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\phi_{t}^{p}}\sum_{i=0}^{t}\prod_{j=i+1}^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})\varphi_{i}\phi_{i}\longrightarrow 0,

(B.3)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\phi_{t}^{p}}\left\{\sum_{i=0}^{t}\prod_{j=i+1}^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})\varphi_{i}\phi_{i}^{p}a_{i}+b\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})\right\}\longrightarrow 0,

where the second result holds for any constant
b
and sequence
\{a_{t}\}_{t}
such that
a_{t}\rightarrow 0
.

Lemma B.4
.

For any scalars
a,b
, we have
P\left(a<{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\leq b\right)\leq b-a
. Furthermore, if
0<a\leq b
, then
P\left(a<{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\leq b\right)\leq b/a-1
.

Lemma B.5
.

Let
A_{t}
,
B_{t}
,
C_{t}
be three variables depending on the index
t
; also let
\Phi(z)=P({\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\leq z)
be the cumulative distribution function of standard Gaussian variable. Suppose for the index
t
,

\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left|P\left(A_{t}\leq z\right)-\Phi(z)\right|\leq a_{t},\quad|B_{t}|\leq b_{t},\quad|C_{t}|\leq c_{t}\quad\text{almost surely}

(B.4)

where
a_{t},b_{t}\geq 0
and
0\leq c_{t}<1
. Then, we have

\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left|P\left(\frac{A_{t}+B_{t}}{\sqrt{1+C_{t}}}\leq z\right)-\Phi(z)\right|\leq a_{t}+b_{t}+\frac{c_{t}}{\sqrt{1-c_{t}}}.

Appendix C
Proofs of Preparation Lemmas

C.1
Proof of Lemma
B.1

By the condition, we know
\varphi_{i-1}/\varphi_{i}=1-\varphi/i+o\left(1/i\right)
. Thus, we have

i\left(1-\varphi_{i-1}^{p}/\varphi_{i}^{p}\right)=i\left(1-\left\{1-\varphi/i+o\left(1/i\right)\right\}^{p}\right)=p\varphi+o(1).

This completes the proof.

C.2
Proof of Lemma
B.2

By Lemma
B.1
, we know for any positive constant
p
,
\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}i\left(1-\varphi_{i-1}^{p}/\varphi_{i}^{p}\right)=p\varphi
. Choosing
p
large enough such that
p\varphi<-1
, the Raabe’s test indicates that
\sum_{i=0}^{\infty}\varphi_{i}^{p}<\infty
. This implies
\varphi_{i}\rightarrow 0
and we complete the proof.

C.3
Proof of Lemma
B.3

For any scalar
A
, we have

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\phi_{t}^{p}}\sum_{i=0}^{t}\prod_{j=i+1}^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})\varphi_{i}\phi_{i}-A

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\phi_{t}^{p}}\prod_{j=0}^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})\left\{\sum_{i=0}^{t}\prod_{j=0}^{i}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})^{-1}\varphi_{i}\phi_{i}-A\phi_{t}^{p}\prod_{j=0}^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})^{-1}\right\}.

For the last term, we have

\displaystyle A\phi_{t}^{p}\prod_{j=0}^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{t}\left(A\phi_{i}^{p}\prod_{j=0}^{i}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})^{-1}-A\phi_{i-1}^{p}\prod_{j=0}^{i-1}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})^{-1}\right)+A\phi_{0}^{p}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{0}\sigma_{k})^{-1}

\displaystyle=\sum_{i=1}^{t}A\phi_{i}^{p}\prod_{j=0}^{i}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})^{-1}\left\{1-\frac{\phi_{i-1}^{p}}{\phi_{i}^{p}}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{i}\sigma_{k})\right\}+A\phi_{0}^{p}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{0}\sigma_{k})^{-1}.

Combining the above two displays, we obtain

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\phi_{t}^{p}}\sum_{i=0}^{t}\prod_{j=i+1}^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})\varphi_{i}\phi_{i}-A

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\phi_{t}^{p}}\prod_{j=0}^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{t}\prod_{j=0}^{i}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})^{-1}\phi_{i}^{p}\left\{\varphi_{i}\phi_{i}^{1-p}-A\left(1-\frac{\phi_{i-1}^{p}}{\phi_{i}^{p}}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{i}\sigma_{k})\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\phi_{0}^{p}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{0}\sigma_{k})^{-1}\left(\varphi_{0}\phi_{0}^{1-p}-A\right)\bigg\}.

(C.1)

We aim to select
A
such that the middle term in (
C.3
) is small. By (
B.1
), we know

\frac{\phi_{i-1}^{p}}{\phi_{i}^{p}}=1-\frac{p\phi}{i}+o\left(\frac{1}{i}\right)=1-\frac{p\phi}{\widetilde{\varphi}}\cdot\varphi_{i}+o(\varphi_{i}),

where the second equality is due to
1/(i\varphi_{i})=1/\widetilde{\varphi}+o(1)
(which is true even if
\widetilde{\varphi}=\penalty\ \infty
). Furthermore, we know

\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{i}\sigma_{k})=1-\varphi_{i}\sum_{k=1}^{l}\sigma_{k}+o(\varphi_{i}).

With these two facts, we have

\displaystyle\varphi_{i}\phi_{i}^{1-p}-A\big\{1-\frac{\phi_{i-1}^{p}}{\phi_{i}^{p}}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{i}\sigma_{k})\big\}=\varphi_{i}\phi_{i}^{1-p}-A\big\{1-(1-\frac{p\phi}{\widetilde{\varphi}}\cdot\varphi_{i}+o(\varphi_{i}))(1-\varphi_{i}\sum_{k=1}^{l}\sigma_{k}+o(\varphi_{i}))\big\}

\displaystyle=\varphi_{i}\phi_{i}^{1-p}-A\left(\frac{p\phi}{\widetilde{\varphi}}+\sum_{k=1}^{l}\sigma_{k}\right)\varphi_{i}+o(\varphi_{i}).

(C.2)

Thus, we let
A=1/(\sum_{k=1}^{l}\sigma_{k}+\phi/\widetilde{\varphi})
if
p=1
and
A=0
if
p\in(0,1)
. Noting that
\phi_{i}^{1-p}\rightarrow 0
, (
C.3
) leads to

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\phi_{t}^{p}}\sum_{i=0}^{t}\prod_{j=i+1}^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{l}

\displaystyle(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})\varphi_{i}\phi_{i}-A

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\phi_{t}^{p}}\prod_{j=0}^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})\bigg\{\sum_{i=1}^{t}\prod_{j=0}^{i}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})^{-1}\phi_{i}^{p}\cdot o(\varphi_{i})

\displaystyle\quad\quad+\phi_{0}^{p}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{0}\sigma_{k})^{-1}\left(\varphi_{0}\phi_{0}^{1-p}-A\right)\bigg\}.

Comparing the above display with (
B.2
) and (
B.3
), we note that the first results in (
B.2
) and (
B.3
) are implied by the second results. Thus, it suffices to prove the second results. We define

\Psi_{t}=\frac{1}{\phi_{t}^{p}}\left\{\sum_{i=0}^{t}\prod_{j=i+1}^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})\varphi_{i}\phi_{i}^{p}a_{i}+b\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{t}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})\right\},

(C.3)

then

\displaystyle\Psi_{t}

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{\phi_{t}^{p}}\left\{\varphi_{t}\phi_{t}^{p}a_{t}+\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{t}\sigma_{k})\left(\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}\prod_{j=i+1}^{t-1}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})\varphi_{i}\phi_{i}^{p}a_{i}+b\cdot\prod_{j=0}^{t-1}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{j}\sigma_{k})\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{pequ:A5}}}}{{=}}\;\;\frac{\phi_{t-1}^{p}}{\phi_{t}^{p}}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{t}\sigma_{k})\Psi_{t-1}+\varphi_{t}a_{t}.

By (
C.3
), we know that

\frac{\phi_{t-1}^{p}}{\phi_{t}^{p}}\prod_{k=1}^{l}(1-\varphi_{t}\sigma_{k})=1-\left(\frac{p\phi}{\widetilde{\varphi}}+\sum_{k=1}^{l}\sigma_{k}\right)\cdot\varphi_{t}+o(\varphi_{t}).

Since
\sum_{k=1}^{l}\sigma_{k}+p\phi/\widetilde{\varphi}>0
, we immediately conclude that for a constant
c>0
and for all large enough
t
,
|\Psi_{t}|\leq(1-c\varphi_{t})|\Psi_{t-1}|+\varphi_{t}|a_{t}|
. Let
t_{1}
be a fixed integer. We apply this inequality recursively and have for any
t\geq t_{1}+1
,

|\Psi_{t}|\leq\prod_{i=t_{1}+1}^{t}(1-c\varphi_{i})|\Psi_{t_{1}}|+\sum_{i=t_{1}+1}^{t}\prod_{j=i+1}^{t}(1-c\varphi_{j})\varphi_{i}|a_{i}|.

For any
\epsilon>0
, since
a_{i}\rightarrow 0
, we select
t_{1}
such that
|a_{i}|\leq\epsilon
, for all
i\geq t_{1}
. Then, the above inequality leads to

\displaystyle|\Psi_{t}|

\displaystyle\leq\prod_{i=t_{1}+1}^{t}(1-c\varphi_{i})|\Psi_{t_{1}}|+\epsilon\sum_{i=t_{1}+1}^{t}\prod_{j=i+1}^{t}(1-c\varphi_{j})\varphi_{i}

\displaystyle=\prod_{i=t_{1}+1}^{t}(1-c\varphi_{i})|\Psi_{t_{1}}|+\frac{\epsilon}{c}\big\{1-\prod_{j=t_{1}+1}^{t}(1-c\varphi_{j})\big\}\leq|\Psi_{t_{1}}|\exp\left(-c\sum_{i=t_{1}+1}^{t}\varphi_{i}\right)+\frac{\epsilon}{c}.

Since
n\varphi_{i}\rightarrow\widetilde{\varphi}\in(0,\infty]
, we know
\sum_{t}\varphi_{t}\rightarrow\infty
. Thus, for the above
\epsilon>0
, there exists
t_{2}\geq t_{1}
such that
|\Psi_{t_{1}}|\exp\left(-c\sum_{i=t_{1}+1}^{t}\varphi_{i}\right)\leq\epsilon/c
,
\forall t\geq t_{2}
, which implies
|\Psi_{t}|\leq 2\epsilon/c
. This means
|\Psi_{t}|\rightarrow 0
and we complete the proof.

C.4
Proof of Lemma
B.4

The first part of statement holds naturally due to the fact that the density of the standard Gaussian satisfies
\exp(-t^{2}/2)/\sqrt{2\pi}\leq 1
for any
t\in\mathbb{R}
. Moreover, for
0<a\leq b
, we have

P\left(a<{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\leq b\right)=\int_{a}^{b}\frac{1}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-t^{2}/2)\;dt\leq\frac{b-a}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-a^{2}/2)\\
=\left(\frac{b}{a}-1\right)\frac{a}{\sqrt{2\pi}}\exp(-a^{2}/2)\leq\frac{b}{a}-1,

where the last inequality uses
a\exp(-a^{2}/2)\leq 1
for all
a
. This completes the proof.

C.5
Proof of Lemma
B.5

We only prove the result for
z>0
. The result of
z\leq 0
can be shown in the same way. We know from (
B.4
) that
\frac{A_{t}-b_{t}}{\sqrt{1+c_{t}}}\leq\frac{A_{t}+B_{t}}{\sqrt{1+C_{t}}}\leq\frac{A_{t}+b_{t}}{\sqrt{1-c_{t}}}
, almost surely. Therefore, we have

\displaystyle P\left(\frac{A_{t}+B_{t}}{\sqrt{1+C_{t}}}\leq z\right)\geq P\left(\frac{A_{t}+b_{t}}{\sqrt{1-c_{t}}}\leq z\right)=P(A_{t}\leq z(1-c_{t})^{1/2}-b_{t})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{aequ:1}}}{{\geq}}\Phi(z(1-c_{t})^{1/2}-b_{t})-a_{t}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{(z\geq 0)}}}{{=}}\;\;\Phi(z)-P\left(z(1-c_{t})^{1/2}-b_{t}<{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\leq z(1-c_{t})^{1/2}\right)-P\left(z(1-c_{t})^{1/2}<{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\leq z\right)-a_{t}

\displaystyle\geq\Phi(z)-b_{t}-\left(\frac{1}{\sqrt{1-c_{t}}}-1\right)-a_{t}\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{by Lemma \ref{aux:lem:3}})

\displaystyle\geq\Phi(z)-b_{t}-\frac{c_{t}}{\sqrt{1-c_{t}}}-a_{t}.

On the other hand, we have

\displaystyle P\left(\frac{A_{t}+B_{t}}{\sqrt{1+C_{t}}}\leq z\right)\leq P\left(\frac{A_{t}-b_{t}}{\sqrt{1+c_{t}}}\leq z\right)=P(A_{t}\leq z(1+c_{t})^{1/2}+b_{t})\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{aequ:1}}}{{\leq}}\Phi(z(1+c_{t})^{1/2}+b_{t})+a_{t}

\displaystyle=\Phi(z)+P\left(z<{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\leq z(1+c_{t})^{1/2}\right)+P\left(z(1+c_{t})^{1/2}<{\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\leq z(1+c_{t})^{1/2}+b_{t}\right)+a_{t}

\displaystyle\leq\Phi(z)+\left\{(1+c_{t})^{1/2}-1\right\}+b_{t}+a_{t}\hskip 9.24994pt(\text{by Lemma \ref{aux:lem:3}})

\displaystyle\leq\Phi(z)+c_{t}+b_{t}+a_{t}.

Combining the above two displays completes the proof.

Appendix D
Proofs of Section
4

D.1
Proof of Lemma
4.4

We note that
\gamma_{S}\leq\|\mathbb{E}[K_{t}S(S^{T}K_{t}^{2}S)^{\dagger}S^{T}K_{t}\mid{\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}]\|\leq\mathbb{E}[\|K_{t}S(S^{T}K_{t}^{2}S)^{\dagger}S^{T}K_{t}\|\mid{\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}]\leq\penalty\ 1
, where the second inequality is by Jensen’s inequality; the third inequality is by the fact that
K_{t}S(S^{T}K_{t}^{2}S)^{\dagger}S^{T}K_{t}
is a projection matrix. This shows (a). Let us define for
j=1,\ldots,\tau
,
C_{t,j}=I-K_{t}S_{t,j}(S_{t,j}^{T}K_{t}^{2}S_{t,j})^{\dagger}S_{t,j}^{T}K_{t}
. Then, we obtain from (
3.4
) that

{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}-{\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{t}=C_{t,\tau-1}({\bm{z}}_{t,\tau-1}-{\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{t})=\left(\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}C_{t,j}\right)({\bm{z}}_{t,0}-{\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{t})=-\left(\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}C_{t,j}\right){\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{t}.

(D.1)

Thus, (b) follows from (
D.1
) and the independence among
\{S_{t,j}\}_{j}
. Moreover, (c) is proved by
(
Gower and Richtárik 2015
, Theorem 4.6)
.

D.2
Proof of Lemma
4.6

By Assumption
4.1
, there exists a constant
\Upsilon_{u}\geq 1
such that

\|\nabla^{2}\mathcal{L}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\lambda}})\|\vee\|\nabla\mathcal{L}({\bm{x}},{\bm{\lambda}})\|\leq\Upsilon_{u},\quad\forall({\bm{x}},{\bm{\lambda}})\in\mathcal{X}\times\Lambda.

(D.2)

By direct calculation, we have (the evaluation point is suppressed for simplicity)

\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}\\
\nabla_{{\bm{\lambda}}}\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}I+\nu\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}^{2}\mathcal{L}&\mu G^{T}\\
\nu G&I\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\mathcal{L}\\
c\end{pmatrix}.

(D.3)

Using (
D.3
) and the definition of
(\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t},\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t})
, we have

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t}\\
\nabla_{{\bm{\lambda}}}\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t}\end{pmatrix}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\\
\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\;

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:AL:der}}}}{{=}}\;\begin{pmatrix}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\\
\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\end{pmatrix}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}I+\nu\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}^{2}\mathcal{L}_{t}&\mu G_{t}^{T}\\
\nu G_{t}&I\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\mathcal{L}_{t}\\
c_{t}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:Newton}}}}{{=}}-\begin{pmatrix}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\\
\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\end{pmatrix}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}I+\nu\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}^{2}\mathcal{L}_{t}&\mu G_{t}^{T}\\
\nu G_{t}&I\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}B_{t}&G_{t}^{T}\\
G_{t}&{\bm{0}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\\
\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle=-\begin{pmatrix}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\\
\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\end{pmatrix}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}B_{t}+\nu\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}^{2}\mathcal{L}_{t}B_{t}+\mu G_{t}^{T}G_{t}&G_{t}^{T}+\nu\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}^{2}\mathcal{L}_{t}G_{t}^{T}\\
G_{t}+\nu G_{t}B_{t}&\nu G_{t}G_{t}^{T}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\\
\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\end{pmatrix}.

Furthermore, using (
D.2
) and Assumption
4.1
, we obtain

\displaystyle\hskip-5.69046pt\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t}\\
\nabla_{{\bm{\lambda}}}\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t}\end{pmatrix}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\\
\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\leq-\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}^{T}B_{t}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}+\nu\Upsilon_{B}\Upsilon_{u}\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}-\mu\|G_{t}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}-2\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}^{T}G_{t}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}+\nu(\Upsilon_{u}+\Upsilon_{B})\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|\|G_{t}^{T}\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\|-\nu\|G_{t}^{T}\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:Newton}}}}{{\leq}}-\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}^{T}B_{t}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}+\nu\Upsilon_{B}\Upsilon_{u}\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}-\mu\|c_{t}\|^{2}+2c_{t}^{T}\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}+\frac{\nu(\Upsilon_{u}+\Upsilon_{B})^{2}}{2}\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}-\frac{\nu}{2}\|G_{t}^{T}\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}^{T}B_{t}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}+\nu(\Upsilon_{B}+\Upsilon_{u})^{2}\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}-\mu\|c_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{8}{\nu\gamma_{G}}\|c_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\nu\gamma_{G}}{8}\|\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip 9.24994pt-\frac{\nu\gamma_{G}}{4}\|\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\|^{2}-\frac{\nu}{4}\|G_{t}^{T}\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\|^{2}\hskip 9.24994pt\text{(Young's inequality and Assumption \ref{ass:1})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:Newton}}}}{{=}}-\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}^{T}B_{t}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}+\nu(\Upsilon_{B}+\Upsilon_{u})^{2}\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}-\left(\mu-\frac{8}{\nu\gamma_{G}}\right)\|c_{t}\|^{2}-\frac{\nu\gamma_{G}}{8}\|\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\|^{2}-\frac{\nu}{4}\|B_{t}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}+\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\mathcal{L}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}^{T}B_{t}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}+\nu(\Upsilon_{B}+\Upsilon_{u})^{2}\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}-\left(\mu-\frac{8}{\nu\gamma_{G}}\right)\|c_{t}\|^{2}-\frac{\nu\gamma_{G}}{8}\|\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\|^{2}-\frac{\nu}{8}\|\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\mathcal{L}_{t}\|^{2}+\frac{\nu\Upsilon_{B}^{2}}{4}\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq-\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}^{T}B_{t}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}+2\nu(\Upsilon_{B}+\Upsilon_{u})^{2}\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}-\left(\mu-\frac{8}{\nu\gamma_{G}}\right)\|c_{t}\|^{2}-\frac{\nu\gamma_{G}}{8}\|\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\|^{2}-\frac{\nu}{8}\|\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\mathcal{L}_{t}\|^{2},

(D.4)

where the second last inequality uses
\|B_{t}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}+\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\mathcal{L}_{t}\|^{2}\geq\|\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\mathcal{L}_{t}\|^{2}/2-\|B_{t}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}\geq\penalty\ \|\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\mathcal{L}_{t}\|^{2}/2\\
-\Upsilon_{B}^{2}\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}
. To further simplify (
D.2
), we decompose the step
\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}
as

\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}=\Delta{\bm{u}}_{t}+\Delta{\bm{v}}_{t},\quad\text{ where }\Delta{\bm{u}}_{t}\in\text{span}(G_{t}^{T})\text{ and }G_{t}\Delta{\bm{v}}_{t}={\bm{0}}.

Then, the first two terms of (
D.2
) can be simplified as

\displaystyle-

\displaystyle\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}^{T}B_{t}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}+2\nu(\Upsilon_{B}+\Upsilon_{u})^{2}\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle=-\Delta{\bm{u}}_{t}^{T}B_{t}\Delta{\bm{u}}_{t}-2\Delta{\bm{u}}_{t}^{T}B_{t}\Delta{\bm{v}}_{t}-\Delta{\bm{v}}_{t}^{T}B_{t}\Delta{\bm{v}}_{t}+2\nu(\Upsilon_{B}+\Upsilon_{u})^{2}\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\Upsilon_{B}\|\Delta{\bm{u}}_{t}\|^{2}+2\Upsilon_{B}\|\Delta{\bm{u}}_{t}\|\|\Delta{\bm{v}}_{t}\|-\gamma_{RH}\|\Delta{\bm{v}}_{t}\|^{2}+2\nu(\Upsilon_{B}+\Upsilon_{u})^{2}\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}\quad\text{(Assumption \ref{ass:1})}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\Upsilon_{B}+\frac{2\Upsilon_{B}^{2}}{\gamma_{RH}}\right)\|\Delta{\bm{u}}_{t}\|^{2}-\frac{\gamma_{RH}}{2}\|\Delta{\bm{v}}_{t}\|^{2}+2\nu(\Upsilon_{B}+\Upsilon_{u})^{2}\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}\quad\text{(Young's inequality)}

\displaystyle=\left(\Upsilon_{B}+\frac{2\Upsilon_{B}^{2}}{\gamma_{RH}}+\frac{\gamma_{RH}}{2}\right)\|\Delta{\bm{u}}_{t}\|^{2}-\left(\frac{\gamma_{RH}}{2}-2\nu(\Upsilon_{B}+\Upsilon_{u})^{2}\right)\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\Upsilon_{B}+\frac{2\Upsilon_{B}^{2}}{\gamma_{RH}}+\frac{\gamma_{RH}}{2}\right)\frac{1}{\gamma_{G}}\|c_{t}\|^{2}-\left(\frac{\gamma_{RH}}{2}-2\nu(\Upsilon_{B}+\Upsilon_{u})^{2}\right)\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2},

where the last inequality uses the fact that
\|c_{t}\|^{2}=\|G_{t}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}=\|G_{t}\Delta{\bm{u}}_{t}\|^{2}\geq\gamma_{G}\|\Delta{\bm{u}}_{t}\|^{2}
. Here, the inequality is due to Assumption
4.1
. Combining the above display with (
D.2
), we have

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t}\\
\nabla_{{\bm{\lambda}}}\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t}\end{pmatrix}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\\
\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\end{pmatrix}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\nu\gamma_{G}}{8}\left\|\begin{pmatrix}\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\\
\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\end{pmatrix}\right\|^{2}-\frac{\nu}{8}\left\|\begin{pmatrix}\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}\mathcal{L}_{t}\\
c_{t}\end{pmatrix}\right\|^{2}-\left(\frac{\gamma_{RH}}{2}-2\nu(\Upsilon_{B}+\Upsilon_{u})^{2}-\frac{\nu\gamma_{G}}{8}\right)\|\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\quad-\left\{\mu-\frac{8}{\nu\gamma_{G}}-\left(\Upsilon_{B}+\frac{2\Upsilon_{B}^{2}}{\gamma_{RH}}+\frac{\gamma_{RH}}{2}\right)\frac{1}{\gamma_{G}}-\frac{\nu}{8}\right\}\|c_{t}\|^{2}.

Thus, choosing
\Upsilon_{1}
large enough (depending only on
\gamma_{G},\gamma_{RH},\Upsilon_{B}
), we complete the proof.

D.3
Proof of Lemma
4.7

Let us denote
{\bm{z}}_{t}=(\Delta{\bm{x}}_{t},\Delta{\bm{\lambda}}_{t})
and recall that
{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}=({\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t},{\bar{\Delta}}{\bm{\lambda}}_{t})
and
{\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{t}=({\widetilde{\Delta}}{\bm{x}}_{t},{\widetilde{\Delta}}{\bm{\lambda}}_{t})
. By Assumption
4.1
and the expression (
D.3
), it is straightforward to see that
\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}
is Lipschitz continuous with a constant
\Upsilon_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{L}}>0
depending on
(\mu,\nu,\Upsilon_{L})
. Thus, using (
3.5
) we have

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t+1}\leq\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t}+{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}(\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t})^{T}{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}+\frac{\Upsilon_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{L}}{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}^{2}}{2}\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\hskip-19.91684pt=\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t}+{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}(\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t})^{T}(I+C_{t}){\bm{z}}_{t}+{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}(\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t})^{T}\left\{{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}-(I+C_{t}){\bm{z}}_{t}\right\}+\frac{\Upsilon_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{L}}{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}^{2}}{2}\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}\right\|^{2},

(D.5)

where
C_{t}
is from Lemma
4.4
(b). By Lemmas
4.6
and
4.4
, the second term can be bounded as

\displaystyle(\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t})^{T}(I+C_{t}){\bm{z}}_{t}

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\nu}{\Upsilon_{1}}\left(\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right)+\|C_{t}\|\left\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t}\right\|\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:AL:der},\eqref{equ:upper:bound}}}}{{\leq}}\;\;\;-\frac{\nu}{\Upsilon_{1}}\left(\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right)+\rho^{\tau}\left(1+(2\nu+\mu)\Upsilon_{u}\right)\left\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\right\|\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq-\frac{\nu}{\Upsilon_{1}}\left(\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right)+2\rho^{\tau}\mu\Upsilon_{u}\left\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\right\|\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq-\left(\frac{\nu}{\Upsilon_{1}}-\rho^{\tau}\mu\Upsilon_{u}\right)\left(\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right),

where the third inequality uses the facts that
\Upsilon_{u}\geq 1
and
1+2\nu\leq\mu
(as long as
\Upsilon_{1}\geq 2
). Thus, we can re-define
\Upsilon_{1}
as
\Upsilon_{1}\leftarrow 2\Upsilon_{1}\Upsilon_{u}
. If
\rho^{\tau}\leq\nu/(\mu\Upsilon_{1})
, then we have

(\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t})^{T}(I+C_{t}){\bm{z}}_{t}\leq-\frac{\nu}{2\Upsilon_{1}}\left(\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right).

(D.6)

Now, we deal with the last two terms of (
D.3
). By Lemma
4.4
(b), we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-2/3}\right]\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[(I+C_{t}){\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:Newton}}}}{{=}}-(I+C_{t})K_{t}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[{\bar{\nabla}}\mathcal{L}_{t}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]=-(I+C_{t})K_{t}^{-1}\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\quad\text{(Assumption \ref{ass:2})}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:Newton}}}}{{=}}(I+C_{t}){\bm{z}}_{t}.

(D.7)

By Lemma
4.4
(b, c), we also have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}-(I+C_{t}){\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 3\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}-{\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]+3\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{t}-{\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]+3\|C_{t}\|^{2}\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 3\rho^{\tau}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]+3\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{t}-{\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]+3\rho^{2\tau}\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}

\displaystyle=3(\rho^{\tau}+\rho^{2\tau})\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}+3(1+\rho^{\tau})\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{t}-{\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\quad(\text{bias-variance decomposition}).

By Assumption
4.1
and
(
Na et al. 2022a
, Lemma 1)
, there exists a constant
\Upsilon_{K}\geq\penalty\ 1
depending on
(\gamma_{G},\gamma_{RH},\Upsilon_{B})
such that
\|K_{t}^{-1}\|\leq\Upsilon_{K}
. Thus, we apply (
3.2
) and (
D.2
), and obtain

\hskip-8.5359pt\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}-(I+C_{t}){\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\leq 3(\rho^{\tau}+\rho^{2\tau})\Upsilon_{K}^{2}\Upsilon_{u}^{2}+3(1+\rho^{\tau})\Upsilon_{K}^{2}\mathbb{E}[\|{\bar{g}}_{t}-\nabla f_{t}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\\
\leq 3(1+\rho^{\tau})\Upsilon_{K}^{2}(\rho^{\tau}\Upsilon_{u}^{2}+\Upsilon_{m})\quad\text{(Assumption \ref{ass:2}\eqref{equ:BM:a})}.

(D.8)

Thus, using (
D.3
) and (
D.8
), we have

\displaystyle\hskip-34.14322pt\mathbb{E}\left[{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}(\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t})^{T}\left\{{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}-(I+C_{t}){\bm{z}}_{t}\right\}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{pequ:4}}}}{{=}}\;\;\mathbb{E}\left[\left\{{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}-(\beta_{t}+\eta_{t})/2\right\}\cdot(\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t})^{T}\left\{{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}-(I+C_{t}){\bm{z}}_{t}\right\}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:sandwich}}}}{{\leq}}\;\frac{\eta_{t}-\beta_{t}}{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t}\right\|\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}-(I+C_{t}){\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:upper:bound}}}}{{\leq}}\;\;\frac{\eta_{t}-\beta_{t}}{2}(1+(2\nu+\mu)\Upsilon_{u})\Upsilon_{u}\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}-(I+C_{t}){\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq(\eta_{t}-\beta_{t})\mu\Upsilon_{u}^{2}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}-(I+C_{t}){\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]}\quad(1\leq\Upsilon_{u}\text{ and }1+2\nu\leq\mu)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{pequ:5}}}}{{\leq}}\;\;2\mu\Upsilon_{K}\Upsilon_{u}^{2}(1+\rho^{\tau})(\sqrt{\Upsilon_{m}}\vee\Upsilon_{u})(\eta_{t}-\beta_{t})\leq 4\mu\Upsilon_{K}\Upsilon_{u}^{2}(\sqrt{\Upsilon_{m}}\vee\Upsilon_{u})(\eta_{t}-\beta_{t}),

(D.9)

and

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\|{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}\|^{2}

\displaystyle\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\;\;\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{pequ:4}}}}{{=}}\;\;\;\left\|(I+C_{t}){\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}-(I+C_{t}){\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:Newton},\eqref{equ:upper:bound}}}}{{\leq}}\;\;(1+\rho^{\tau})^{2}\Upsilon_{K}^{2}\Upsilon_{u}^{2}+\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}-(I+C_{t}){\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\quad(\text{also use Lemma \ref{lem:1}(b)})

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{pequ:5}}}}{{\leq}}(1+\rho^{\tau})^{2}\Upsilon_{K}^{2}\Upsilon_{u}^{2}+3(1+\rho^{\tau})\Upsilon_{K}^{2}(\rho^{\tau}\Upsilon_{u}^{2}+\Upsilon_{m})\leq 16\Upsilon_{K}^{2}(\Upsilon_{u}^{2}\vee\Upsilon_{m}).

(D.10)

Combining (
D.3
) with (
D.3
) and (
D.6
), plugging into (
D.3
), and using (
3.6
), we obtain

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t+1}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\leq\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t}-\frac{\nu\beta_{t}}{2\Upsilon_{1}}\left(\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t}\right\|^{2}+\left\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\right\|^{2}\right)\\
+4\mu\Upsilon_{K}\Upsilon_{u}^{2}(\sqrt{\Upsilon_{m}}\vee\Upsilon_{u})(\eta_{t}-\beta_{t})+8\Upsilon_{\mathcal{A}\mathcal{L}}\Upsilon_{K}^{2}(\Upsilon_{u}^{2}\vee\Upsilon_{m})\eta_{t}^{2}.

Choosing
\Upsilon_{2}
large enough that depends on
(\mu,\nu,\gamma_{G},\gamma_{RH},\Upsilon_{B},\Upsilon_{m},\Upsilon_{L})
, and noting that
(\mu,\nu)
are determined by
(\gamma_{G},\gamma_{RH},\Upsilon_{B})
, we complete the proof.

D.4
Proof of Theorem
4.8

Note that the condition of
\tau
in the statement implies that we can select
(\mu,\nu)
to satisfy the condition in Lemma
4.6
and have
\rho^{\tau}\leq\nu/(\mu\Upsilon_{1})
with
\rho=1-\gamma_{S}
. Thus, Lemma
4.7
leads to

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t+1}-\min_{\mathcal{X}\times\Lambda}\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}]\leq\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t}-\min_{\mathcal{X}\times\Lambda}\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}-\frac{\nu\beta_{t}}{2\Upsilon_{1}}\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\|^{2}+\Upsilon_{2}(\chi_{t}+\eta_{t}^{2}).

By Robbins-Siegmund theorem (see
Robbins and Siegmund 1971
or
(
Duflo 1997
, Theorem 1.3.12)
), we conclude that
\sum_{t}\beta_{t}\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\|^{2}<\infty
. Since
\sum_{t}\beta_{t}=\infty
from (
4.4
), we know that
\liminf_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\|=0
. Furthermore, we note that

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|({\bm{x}}_{t+1}-{\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t+1}-{\bm{\lambda}}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]=\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|({\bm{x}}_{t+1}-{\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t+1}-{\bm{\lambda}}_{t})\right\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\right]\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:update}}}{{\leq}}\eta_{t}^{2}\mathbb{E}\left[\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}\right\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{t-1}\right]\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{pequ:7}}}{{\leq}}16\Upsilon_{K}^{2}(\Upsilon_{u}^{2}\vee\Upsilon_{m})\cdot\eta_{t}^{2}.

(D.11)

Summing over
t=1
to
\infty
, exchanging the expectation and summation by applying Fubini’s theorem
(
Durrett 2019
, Theorem 1.7.2)
, and noting that
\sum_{t}\eta_{t}^{2}<\infty
, we obtain

\mathbb{E}\left[\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\left\|({\bm{x}}_{t+1}-{\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t+1}-{\bm{\lambda}}_{t})\right\|^{2}\right]<\infty.

This implies
\sum_{t=1}^{\infty}\|({\bm{x}}_{t+1}-{\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t+1}-{\bm{\lambda}}_{t})\|<\infty
almost surely and, thus,
\|({\bm{x}}_{t+1}-{\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t+1}-{\bm{\lambda}}_{t})\|\rightarrow 0
as
t\rightarrow\infty
almost surely. Suppose for any run of the algorithm
\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\|\neq 0
, then we have
\limsup_{t\rightarrow\infty}\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\|=\epsilon>0
. Then, there exist two index sequences
\{t_{1,i}\}_{i},\{t_{2,i}\}_{i}
with
t_{1,i+1}>t_{2,i}>t_{1,i}
such that, for all
i=1,2,\ldots
,

\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t_{1,i}}\|\geq\epsilon/2,\quad\quad\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{j}\|\geq\epsilon/3\;\text{ for }j=t_{1,i}+1,\ldots,t_{2,i}-1,\quad\quad\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t_{2,i}}\|<\epsilon/3.

(D.12)

Since
\sum_{t}\beta_{t}\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\|^{2}<\infty
, we know

\infty>\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=t_{1,i}}^{t_{2,i}-1}\beta_{j}\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{j}\|^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{pequ:8}}}{{\geq}}\frac{\epsilon^{2}}{9}\sum_{i=1}^{\infty}\sum_{j=t_{1,i}}^{t_{2,i}-1}\beta_{j}.

(D.13)

Furthermore, by (
D.11
), we have

\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|({\bm{x}}_{t_{2,i}}-{\bm{x}}_{t_{1,i}},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t_{2,i}}-{\bm{\lambda}}_{t_{1,i}})\right\|\right]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{pequ:33}}}{{\leq}}4\Upsilon_{K}(\Upsilon_{u}\vee\sqrt{\Upsilon_{m}})\sum_{j=t_{1,i}}^{t_{2,i}-1}\eta_{j}\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:sandwich}}}{{=}}4\Upsilon_{K}(\Upsilon_{u}\vee\sqrt{\Upsilon_{m}})\left\{\sum_{j=t_{1,i}}^{t_{2,i}-1}\beta_{j}+\sum_{j=t_{1,i}}^{t_{2,i}-1}\chi_{j}\right\}.

Summing over
i=1
to
\infty
, and noting that
\sum_{i}\sum_{j=t_{1,i}}^{t_{2,i}-1}\beta_{j}<\infty
by (
D.13
) and
\sum_{i}\sum_{j=t_{1,i}}^{t_{2,i}-1}\chi_{j}\leq\sum_{j=1}^{\infty}\chi_{j}<\infty
, we exchange the expectation and summation by applying Fubini’s theorem again. We know that the sequence
\{({\bm{x}}_{t_{2,i}}-{\bm{x}}_{t_{1,i}},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t_{2,i}}-{\bm{\lambda}}_{t_{1,i}})\}_{i}
converges to zero as
i\rightarrow\penalty\ \infty
with probability one. This contradicts with
\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t_{1,i}}\|\geq\epsilon/2
and
\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t_{2,i}}\|<\epsilon/3
in (
D.12
). We complete the proof.

D.5
Proof of Corollary
4.9

Applying Lemma
4.7
and taking full expectation, we know for some constants
h_{1},h_{2}>0
,

\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t+1}]\leq\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t}]-h_{1}\beta_{t}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\|^{2}]+h_{2}(\chi_{t}+\eta_{t}^{2}),\quad\forall t\geq 0.

Rearranging the inequality and summing over
t=0
to
{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1
, we obtain

\displaystyle h_{1}\sum_{t=0}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\|^{2}]\leq\sum_{t=0}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}\frac{1}{\beta_{t}}\left((\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t}]-\min_{\mathcal{X}\times\Lambda}\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu})-(\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t+1}]-\min_{\mathcal{X}\times\Lambda}\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu})\right)+h_{2}\sum_{t=0}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}\frac{\chi_{t}+\eta_{t}^{2}}{\beta_{t}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{0}]-\min_{\mathcal{X}\times\Lambda}\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}}{\beta_{0}}+\sum_{t=1}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}\left(\frac{1}{\beta_{t}}-\frac{1}{\beta_{t-1}}\right)(\mathbb{E}[\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}^{t}]-\min_{\mathcal{X}\times\Lambda}\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu})+h_{2}\sum_{t=0}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}\frac{\chi_{t}+\eta_{t}^{2}}{\beta_{t}}.

Denoting
\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}=\max_{\mathcal{X}\times\Lambda}\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}-\min_{\mathcal{X}\times\Lambda}\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}
, we further have

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pth_{1}\sum_{t=0}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\|^{2}]\leq\left(\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}\vee h_{2}\right)\left\{\frac{1}{\beta_{0}}+\sum_{t=1}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}\left(\frac{1}{\beta_{t}}-\frac{1}{\beta_{t-1}}\right)+\sum_{t=0}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}\frac{\chi_{t}+\eta_{t}^{2}}{\beta_{t}}\right\}

\displaystyle=\left(\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}\vee h_{2}\right)\left\{\frac{1}{\beta_{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}}+\sum_{t=0}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}\frac{\chi_{t}+\eta_{t}^{2}}{\beta_{t}}\right\}=\left(\Delta\mathcal{L}_{\mu,\nu}\vee h_{2}\right)\left\{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{a}+\sum_{t=0}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}\frac{\chi_{t}+\eta_{t}^{2}}{\beta_{t}}\right\}.

For the last term on the right hand side, we have

\displaystyle\sum_{t=0}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}\frac{\chi_{t}+\eta_{t}^{2}}{\beta_{t}}

\displaystyle=\sum_{t=0}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}\left\{(t+1)^{a-b}+(t+1)^{a}\left((t+1)^{-2a}+2(t+1)^{-(a+b)}+(t+1)^{-2b}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\sum_{t=0}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}(t+1)^{a-b}+4\sum_{t=0}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}(t+1)^{-a}=5+\sum_{t=1}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}\left\{(t+1)^{a-b}+4(t+1)^{-a}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq 5+\int_{0}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}(t+1)^{a-b}+4(t+1)^{-a}dt\quad(\text{by the convexity of }x^{p}\text{ with }p<0)

\displaystyle\leq\begin{cases}5+\frac{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{1+a-b}}{1+a-b}+\frac{4{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{1-a}}{1-a}&\text{ if } $1+a>b$,\\
5+\log({\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon})+\frac{4{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{1-a}}{1-a}&\text{ if } $1+a=b$,\\
5+\frac{1}{b-a-1}+\frac{4{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{1-a}}{1-a}&\text{ if } $1+a<b$.\end{cases}

Combining the above two displays, dividing
{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}
on both sides, and using “
\lesssim
" to neglect constant factors (i.e., not depending on
{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}
), we obtain

\displaystyle\epsilon^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\left(\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}}\sum_{t=0}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\|]\right)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}}\sum_{t=0}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}\left(\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\|]\right)^{2}\leq\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}}\sum_{t=0}^{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}-1}\mathbb{E}[\|\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}\|^{2}]

\displaystyle\lesssim\begin{cases}\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{1-a}}+\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{b-a}}+\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{a}}&\text{ if } $1+a>b$,\\
\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{1-a}}+\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{a}}&\text{ if } $1+a=b$,\\
\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{1-a}}+\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{a}}&\text{ if } $1+a<b$,\\
\end{cases}\quad(\text{use }1/{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}\leq 1/{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{a}\text{ and }\log({\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon})/{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}\lesssim 1/{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{a}),

\displaystyle\lesssim\begin{cases}\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{b-a}}+\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{a}}&\text{ if } $1>b$,\\
\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{1-a}}+\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{a}}&\text{ if } $1\leq b$,\end{cases}=\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{(1\wedge b)-a}}+\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{a}}\lesssim\frac{1}{{\mathcal{T}}_{\epsilon}^{a\wedge(1-a)\wedge(b-a)}}.

This completes the proof.

Appendix E
Proofs of Section
5

E.1
Proof of Lemma
5.1

For notational brevity, we let
\bm{\omega}_{t}=({\bm{x}}_{t}-{\bm{x}}^{\star},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}-{\bm{\lambda}}^{\star})
. By the scheme of Algorithm
1
, we have

\displaystyle\bm{\omega}_{t+1}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:update}}}}{{=}}\bm{\omega}_{t}+{\bar{\alpha}}_{t}{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}=\bm{\omega}_{t}+\varphi_{t}{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}+\left({\bar{\alpha}}_{t}-\varphi_{t}\right){\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}

\displaystyle=\bm{\omega}_{t}+\varphi_{t}(I+C_{t}){\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{t}+\varphi_{t}\left\{{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}-(I+C_{t}){\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{t}\right\}+\left({\bar{\alpha}}_{t}-\varphi_{t}\right){\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:Newton}}}}{{=}}\bm{\omega}_{t}-\varphi_{t}(I+C_{t})K_{t}^{-1}{\bar{\nabla}}\mathcal{L}_{t}+\varphi_{t}\left\{{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}-(I+C_{t}){\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{t}\right\}+\left({\bar{\alpha}}_{t}-\varphi_{t}\right){\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}

\displaystyle=\bm{\omega}_{t}-\varphi_{t}(I+C_{t})K_{t}^{-1}\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}-\varphi_{t}(I+C_{t})K_{t}^{-1}({\bar{\nabla}}\mathcal{L}_{t}-\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t})+\varphi_{t}\left\{{\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}-(I+C_{t}){\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{t}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad+\left({\bar{\alpha}}_{t}-\varphi_{t}\right){\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{rec:def:b}}}}{{=}}\;\bm{\omega}_{t}-\varphi_{t}(I+C_{t})K_{t}^{-1}\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}+\varphi_{t}\bm{\theta}^{t}+\left({\bar{\alpha}}_{t}-\varphi_{t}\right){\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}

\displaystyle=\bm{\omega}_{t}-\varphi_{t}(I+C_{t})(K^{\star})^{-1}\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}-\varphi_{t}(I+C_{t})\left\{K_{t}^{-1}-(K^{\star})^{-1}\right\}\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}+\varphi_{t}\bm{\theta}^{t}+\left({\bar{\alpha}}_{t}-\varphi_{t}\right){\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{rec:def:d}}}}{{=}}\;\left\{I-\varphi_{t}(I+C_{t})\right\}\bm{\omega}_{t}-\varphi_{t}(I+C_{t})(K^{\star})^{-1}\bm{\psi}^{t}-\varphi_{t}(I+C_{t})\left\{K_{t}^{-1}-(K^{\star})^{-1}\right\}\nabla\mathcal{L}_{t}

\displaystyle\quad+\varphi_{t}\bm{\theta}^{t}+\left({\bar{\alpha}}_{t}-\varphi_{t}\right){\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{rec:def:c}}}}{{=}}\;\;\left\{I-\varphi_{t}(I+C^{\star})\right\}\bm{\omega}_{t}+\varphi_{t}(\bm{\theta}^{t}+\bm{\delta}^{t})+\left({\bar{\alpha}}_{t}-\varphi_{t}\right){\bm{z}}_{t,\tau}.

We apply the above equation recursively and show the result. Moreover, under
Assumptions

4.2
and
4.3
, we know
\mathbb{E}[{\bar{g}}_{i}-\nabla f_{i}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]={\bm{0}}
and, by (
D.3
),
\mathbb{E}\left[{\bm{z}}_{i,\tau}-(I+C_{i}){\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{i}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]={\bm{0}}
. Thus,
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\theta}^{i}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]={\bm{0}}
and
\bm{\theta}^{i}
is a martingale difference.

E.2
Proof of Lemma
5.2

Let us denote
\text{rank}(S)=r
. Since
K_{t}
,
K^{\star}
have full rank,
\text{rank}(K_{t}S)=\text{rank}(K^{\star}S)=r
. Let
K^{\star}S=EDF^{T}
be the truncated singular value decomposition of
K^{\star}S
. We have

E\in\mathbb{R}^{(d+m)\times r},\hskip 9.24994ptF\in\mathbb{R}^{q\times r},\hskip 9.24994ptE^{T}E=F^{T}F=I,\hskip 9.24994ptD={\rm diag}(D_{1},\ldots,D_{r})\;\;\text{ with }D_{1}\geq\ldots\geq D_{r}>0.

Similarly, we let
K_{t}S=E^{\prime}D^{\prime}(F^{\prime})^{T}
. By direct calculation, we have

\|K_{t}S(S^{T}K_{t}^{2}S)^{\dagger}S^{T}K_{t}-K^{\star}S(S^{T}(K^{\star})^{2}S)^{\dagger}S^{T}K^{\star}\|=\|EE^{T}-E^{\prime}(E^{\prime})^{T}\|.

(E.1)

Define the principle angles
\theta_{p}
between
\text{span}(E)
and
\text{span}(E^{\prime})
to be
\theta_{p}=(\theta_{p,1},\ldots,\theta_{p,r})
, so that
E^{T}E^{\prime}
has the singular value decomposition
E^{T}E^{\prime}=P\cos(\theta_{p})Q^{T}
, where
P,Q\in\mathbb{R}^{r\times r}
are orthonormal matrices and
\cos(\theta_{p})={\rm diag}(\cos(\theta_{p,1}),\ldots,\cos(\theta_{p,r}))
(similar for
\sin(\theta_{p})
). We
further
let
E^{\perp}\in\mathbb{R}^{(d+m)\times(d+m-r)}
be the complement of
E
, and express
E^{\prime}
as

E^{\prime}=EA+E^{\perp}B.

(E.2)

Then,
E^{T}E^{\prime}=A=P\cos(\theta_{p})Q^{T}
and
I=(E^{\prime})^{T}E^{\prime}=A^{T}A+B^{T}B
. By the above formulation,

\displaystyle\|EE^{T}-E^{\prime}(E^{\prime})^{T}\|\;\;

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{UU:2}}}}{{=}}\;\;\left\|(E,E^{\perp})\begin{pmatrix}I-AA^{T}&-AB^{T}\\
-BA^{T}&-BB^{T}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}E^{T}\\
(E^{\perp})^{T}\end{pmatrix}\right\|=\left\|\begin{pmatrix}I-AA^{T}&-AB^{T}\\
-BA^{T}&-BB^{T}\end{pmatrix}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\left\|\begin{pmatrix}I-AA^{T}&{\bm{0}}\\
{\bm{0}}&-BB^{T}\end{pmatrix}\right\|+\left\|\begin{pmatrix}{\bm{0}}&AB^{T}\\
BA^{T}&{\bm{0}}\end{pmatrix}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\max\{\|I-AA^{T}\|,\|BB^{T}\|\}+\|AB^{T}\|

\displaystyle=\max\{\|I-AA^{T}\|,\|I-A^{T}A\|\}+\|AB^{T}\|

\displaystyle=\|\sin(\theta_{p})\|^{2}+\sqrt{\|P\cos(\theta_{p})\sin^{2}(\theta_{p})\cos(\theta_{p})P^{T}\|}

\displaystyle=\|\sin(\theta_{p})\|^{2}+\|\sin(\theta_{p})\cos(\theta_{p})\|\leq 2\|\sin(\theta_{p})\|.

(E.3)

On the other hand, by Wedin’s
\sin(\Theta)
theorem
(
Wedin 1972
, (3.1))
, we know

\|\sin(\theta_{p})\|\leq\frac{\|(K^{\star}-K_{t})S\|}{D_{r}}.

(E.4)

We let
F_{r}
be the
r
-th column of
F
and have
D_{r}^{2}=F_{r}^{T}S^{T}(K^{\star})^{2}SF_{r}\geq(\sigma_{\min}(K^{\star}))^{2}F_{r}^{T}S^{T}SF_{r}
. Since
\text{kernel}(K^{\star}S)=\text{kernel}(S)
and
F_{r}\in\text{kernel}^{\perp}(K^{\star}S)
, we know
F_{r}\in\text{kernel}^{\perp}(S)=\penalty\ \text{span}(S^{T})
. Thus,
F_{r}^{T}S^{T}SF_{r}\geq\lambda_{\min}^{+}(S^{T}S)
, where
\lambda_{\min}^{+}(S^{T}S)=(\sigma_{\min}^{+}(S))^{2}
is the least positive eigenvalue of
S^{T}S
. Therefore, we have

D_{r}\geq\sigma_{\min}(K^{\star})\sigma_{\min}^{+}(S).

(E.5)

Combining all above derivations, we obtain

\|K_{t}S(S^{T}K_{t}^{2}S)^{\dagger}S^{T}K_{t}-K^{\star}S(S^{T}(K^{\star})^{2}S)^{\dagger}S^{T}K^{\star}\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{UU:1}}}{{=}}\|EE^{T}-E^{\prime}(E^{\prime})^{T}\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{UU:3}}}{{\leq}}2\|\sin(\theta_{p})\|\\
\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{UU:4}}}{{\leq}}\frac{2\|K_{t}-K^{\star}\|\cdot\|S\|}{D_{r}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{UU:5}}}{{\leq}}\frac{2\|K_{t}-K^{\star}\|}{\sigma_{\min}(K^{\star})}\cdot\frac{\|S\|}{\sigma_{\min}^{+}(S)}.

This completes the proof.

E.3
Proof of Corollary
5.4

Denote
A_{t}=I-\mathbb{E}[K_{t}S(S^{T}K_{t}^{2}S)^{\dagger}S^{T}K_{t}\mid{\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}]
and
A^{\star}=I-\mathbb{E}[K^{\star}S(S^{T}(K^{\star})^{2}S)^{\dagger}S^{T}K^{\star}]
. We have

\displaystyle\left\|C_{t}-C^{\star}\right\|

\displaystyle=\left\|A_{t}^{\tau}-(A^{\star})^{\tau}\right\|\leq\left\|A_{t}^{\tau-1}(A_{t}-A^{\star})\right\|+\left\|(A_{t}^{\tau-1}-(A^{\star})^{\tau-1})A^{\star}\right\|

\displaystyle\leq\|A_{t}-A^{\star}\|+\left\|A_{t}^{\tau-1}-(A^{\star})^{\tau-1}\right\|\quad(\|A_{t}\|\vee\|A^{\star}\|\leq 1)

\displaystyle\leq\tau\|A_{t}-A^{\star}\|\leq\tau\mathbb{E}\left[\left\|K_{t}S(S^{T}K_{t}^{2}S)^{\dagger}S^{T}K_{t}-K^{\star}S(S^{T}(K^{\star})^{2}S)^{\dagger}S^{T}K^{\star}\right\|\mid{\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t}\right]

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2\tau\|K_{t}-K^{\star}\|}{\sigma_{\min}(K^{\star})}\mathbb{E}\left[\|S\|\|S^{\dagger}\|\right]\leq\frac{2\tau\Upsilon_{S}}{\sigma_{\min}(K^{\star})}\left\|K_{t}-K^{\star}\right\|\quad(\text{by Assumption \ref{ass:5}}).

This completes the proof.

E.4
Proof of Theorem
5.5

We present some lemmas that bound
\mathcal{I}_{1,t}
,
\mathcal{I}_{2,t}
, and
\mathcal{I}_{3,t}
in (
5.2a
), (
5.2b
), and (
5.2c
),
respectively
. The proofs of these lemmas are presented in Appendix
E.4.1
–
E.4.4
.

Lemma E.1
.

Under Assumptions
4.1
,
4.2
(
4.2a
,
4.2e
),
4.3
, and
({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t})\rightarrow({\bm{x}}^{\star},{\bm{\lambda}}^{\star})
, suppose

\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}t\left(1-\varphi_{t-1}/\varphi_{t}\right)=\varphi<0,\quad\quad\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}t\varphi_{t}=\widetilde{\varphi}\in(0,\infty],\quad\quad 1.5(1-\rho^{\tau})+\varphi/\widetilde{\varphi}>0.

(E.6)

Then, for any
\upsilon>0
,

\mathcal{I}_{1,t}=o\big(\sqrt{\varphi_{t}\{\log(1/\varphi_{t})\}^{1+\upsilon}}\;\big)\quad\text{ a.s}.

(E.7)

Furthermore, if (
4.2a
) is strengthened to (
4.2b
), then we have

(a):

(asymptotic rate)
\mathcal{I}_{1,t}=O(\sqrt{\varphi_{t}\log(1/\varphi_{t})})
a.s.

(b):

(asymptotic normality)
\sqrt{1/\varphi_{t}}\cdot\mathcal{I}_{1,t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle d}}{{\longrightarrow}}{\mathcal{N}}(0,\Xi^{\star})
where
\Xi^{\star}
is from (
5.11
).

(c):

(Berry-Esseen bound) For any vector
\bm{w}=(\bm{w}_{{\bm{x}}},\bm{w}_{{\bm{\lambda}}})\in\mathbb{R}^{d+m}
such that
\bm{w}^{T}\Xi^{\star}\bm{w}\neq 0
,

\sup_{z\in\mathbb{R}}\left|P\left(\frac{\sqrt{1/\varphi_{t}}\cdot\bm{w}^{T}\mathcal{I}_{1,t}}{\sqrt{\bm{w}^{T}\Xi^{\star}\bm{w}}}\leq z\right)-P\left({\mathcal{N}}(0,1)\leq z\right)\right|=O\big(\sqrt{\varphi_{t}}\log(1/\varphi_{t})\;\big).

Lemma E.2
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
E.1
with (
4.2a
) and assume for the adaptivity gap
\chi_{t}
that for some
p,q\in(0,1]
,

\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}t\left(1-\chi_{t-1}/\chi_{t}\right)=\chi<\varphi,\;\;(1-\rho^{\tau})+p(\chi-0.5\varphi)/\widetilde{\varphi}>0,\;\;(1-\rho^{\tau})+q(\chi-\varphi)/\widetilde{\varphi}>0.

(E.8)

Then, for any
\nu>0
(if
q<1
, the second
O(\cdot)
can be strengthened to
o(\cdot)
)

\mathcal{I}_{2,t}=o\big(\chi_{t}^{p}/\varphi_{t}^{0.5p}\sqrt{\{\log(1/\chi_{t})\}^{1+\nu}}\;\big)+O(\chi_{t}^{q}/\varphi_{t}^{q})\quad\text{ a.s.}

Furthermore, if (
4.2a
) is strengthened to (
4.2b
), then we have (if
p<1
, the first
O(\cdot)
can also be strengthened to
o(\cdot)
)

\mathcal{I}_{2,t}=O\big(\chi_{t}^{p}/\varphi_{t}^{0.5p}\sqrt{\log(1/\chi_{t})}\;\big)+O(\chi_{t}^{q}/\varphi_{t}^{q})\quad\text{ a.s.}

Lemma E.3
.

Under the conditions of Lemma
E.2
with (
4.2a
), we have for any
\upsilon>0
,

\mathcal{I}_{3,t}=o\big(\sqrt{\varphi_{t}\{\log(1/\varphi_{t})\}^{1+\upsilon}}\;\big)+o\big(\chi_{t}^{p}/\varphi_{t}^{0.5p}\sqrt{\{\log(1/\chi_{t})\}^{1+\nu}}\big)+o(\chi_{t}^{q}/\varphi_{t}^{q})=o(\mathcal{I}_{1,t}+\mathcal{I}_{2,t})\quad\text{a.s.}

If (
4.2a
) is strengthened to (
4.2b
), the above result holds with
\upsilon=0
.

We apply the above lemmas. We first check the conditions (
E.6
) and (
E.8
). Since
\varphi_{t}=(\beta_{t}+\eta_{t})/2=\beta_{t}+\chi_{t}/2
and
\chi_{t}=o(\beta_{t})
(as implied by
\chi<\beta
), we know
\beta_{t}\leq\varphi_{t}\leq\beta_{t}+o(\beta_{t})
and
\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}t\varphi_{t}=\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}t\beta_{t}=\widetilde{\beta}
. Furthermore, we have

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}

\displaystyle t\left(1-\frac{\varphi_{t-1}}{\varphi_{t}}\right)=\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}t\left(1-\frac{\beta_{t-1}}{\beta_{t}}+\frac{\beta_{t-1}}{\beta_{t}}\left\{1-\frac{2+\chi_{t-1}/\beta_{t-1}}{2+\chi_{t}/\beta_{t}}\right\}\right)

\displaystyle=\beta+\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}t\left(1-\frac{2+\chi_{t-1}/\beta_{t-1}}{2+\chi_{t}/\beta_{t}}\right)=\beta+\frac{1}{2}\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}t\left(\frac{\chi_{t}}{\beta_{t}}-\frac{\chi_{t-1}}{\beta_{t-1}}\right)\quad(\text{since }\chi_{t}=o(\beta_{t}))

\displaystyle=\beta+\frac{1}{2}\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\chi_{t}}{\beta_{t}}\cdot t\left(1-\frac{\chi_{t-1}}{\chi_{t}}\cdot\frac{\beta_{t}}{\beta_{t-1}}\right)=\beta+\frac{\chi-\beta}{2}\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}\frac{\chi_{t}}{\beta_{t}}=\beta.

The above derivations show that
\varphi=\beta
and
\widetilde{\varphi}=\widetilde{\beta}
. Thus, (
5.7
) implies (
E.6
) holds. Moreover, for any constant
p\in(0,1]
such that
(1-\rho^{\tau})+p(\chi-0.5\beta)/\widetilde{\beta}=(1-\rho^{\tau})+p(\chi-0.5\varphi)/\widetilde{\varphi}>0
, we simply let
q=p
and have
(1-\rho^{\tau})+q(\chi-\varphi)/\widetilde{\varphi}>0
. Thus, (
E.8
) holds with
q=p
. We note that for any
\nu\geq 0
,

\displaystyle\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}t\left(1-\frac{\varphi_{t-1}^{0.5p}\{\log(1/\chi_{t-1})\}^{0.5(1+\nu)}}{\varphi_{t}^{0.5p}\{\log(1/\chi_{t})\}^{0.5(1+\nu)}}\right)\quad\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\text{Lem. \ref{aux:lem:5}}}}}{{=}}\quad\;0.5p\varphi+0.5(1+\nu)\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}t\left(1-\frac{\log(1/\chi_{t-1})}{\log(1/\chi_{t})}\right)

\displaystyle=0.5p\varphi+0.5(1+\nu)\lim\limits_{t\rightarrow\infty}t\left(\frac{\log(\chi_{t-1}/\chi_{t})}{\log(1/\chi_{t})}\right)=0.5p\varphi+0.5(1+\nu)\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}t\left(\frac{\frac{\chi_{t-1}-\chi_{t}}{\chi_{t}}+O\left(\frac{(\chi_{t-1}-\chi_{t})^{2}}{\chi_{t}^{2}}\right)}{\log(1/\chi_{t})}\right)

\displaystyle=0.5p\varphi-0.5(1+\nu)\chi\lim_{t\rightarrow\infty}1/\log(1/\chi_{t})=0.5p\varphi<0\quad\text{(by \eqref{cond:varphi} and $\chi_{t}\rightarrow 0$)}.

Thus, by Lemma
B.2
, we know
\chi_{t}^{p}/\varphi_{t}^{0.5p}\sqrt{\{\log(1/\chi_{t})\}^{1+\nu}}=o(\chi_{t}^{p}/\varphi_{t}^{p})
. The convergence rate of
({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t})
comes from Lemmas
5.1
,
E.1
,
E.2
,
E.3
, the above fact, and the fact that
\beta_{t}\leq\varphi_{t}\leq 2\beta_{t}
. We complete the proof.

E.4.1
Proof of Lemma
E.1

We need a preparation lemma. Recall that we suppose
({\bm{x}}^{\star},{\bm{\lambda}}^{\star})
is a local solution of (
1.1
) with
G^{\star}
being full row rank and
\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}^{2}\mathcal{L}^{\star}
being positive definite in the null space
\{{\bm{x}}\in\mathbb{R}^{d}:G^{\star}{\bm{x}}={\bm{0}}\}
.

Lemma E.4
.

Under Assumptions
4.1
,
4.2
(
4.2d
) and
({\bm{x}}_{t},{\bm{\lambda}}_{t})\rightarrow({\bm{x}}^{\star},{\bm{\lambda}}^{\star})
, we have
\frac{1}{t}\sum_{i=0}^{t-1}{\bar{\nabla}}_{{\bm{x}}}^{2}\mathcal{L}_{i}\\
\rightarrow\nabla_{{\bm{x}}}^{2}\mathcal{L}^{\star}
as
t\rightarrow\infty
. Further, with a small
\gamma_{RH}
and a large
\Upsilon_{B}
,
\Delta_{t}={\bm{0}}
for all large enough
t
.

Let
I+C^{\star}=U\Sigma U^{T}
with
\Sigma={\rm diag}(\sigma_{1},\ldots,\sigma_{d+m})
be the eigenvalue decomposition. Then,

\mathcal{I}_{1,t}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{rec:a}}}{{=}}\sum_{i=0}^{t}\prod_{j=i+1}^{t}\left\{I-\varphi_{j}(I+C^{\star})\right\}\varphi_{i}\bm{\theta}^{i}=U\sum_{i=0}^{t}\prod_{j=i+1}^{t}\left\{I-\varphi_{j}\Sigma\right\}\varphi_{i}U^{T}\bm{\theta}^{i}.

(E.9)

Since
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\theta}^{i}|\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]={\bm{0}}
, we aim to apply the strong law of large number
(
Duflo 1997
, Theorem 1.3.15)
, the central limit theorem
(
Duflo 1997
, Corollary 2.1.10)
, and the Berry-Esseen inequality
(
Fan 2019
, Theorem 2.1)
to show each result in the lemma. We
compute
the
conditional
covariance of
\mathcal{I}_{1,t}
, which is defined as
(
Duflo 1997
, Proposition 1.3.7)

\langle\mathcal{I}_{1}\rangle_{t}\coloneqq U\sum_{i=0}^{t}\prod_{j=i+1}^{t}\left\{I-\varphi_{j}\Sigma\right\}\varphi_{i}^{2}U^{T}\mathbb{E}[\bm{\theta}^{i}(\bm{\theta}^{i})^{T}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]U\big(\prod_{j=i+1}^{t}\left\{I-\varphi_{j}\Sigma\right\}\big)^{T}U^{T}.

(E.10)

For the term
\mathbb{E}[\bm{\theta}^{i}(\bm{\theta}^{i})^{T}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]
, we note that

\displaystyle\hskip-28.45274pt\mathbb{E}[\bm{\theta}^{i}(\bm{\theta}^{i})^{T}

\displaystyle\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{rec:def:b}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}\big[\left\{(I+C_{i})K_{i}^{-1}({\bar{\nabla}}\mathcal{L}_{i}-\nabla\mathcal{L}_{i})-\left\{{\bm{z}}_{i,\tau}-(I+C_{i}){\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{i}\right\}\right\}

\displaystyle\quad\quad\left\{(I+C_{i})K_{i}^{-1}({\bar{\nabla}}\mathcal{L}_{i}-\nabla\mathcal{L}_{i})-\left\{{\bm{z}}_{i,\tau}-(I+C_{i}){\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{i}\right\}\right\}^{T}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\big]

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{pequ:4}}}}{{=}}\;\;(I+C_{i})K_{i}^{-1}\mathbb{E}\left[({\bar{\nabla}}\mathcal{L}_{i}-\nabla\mathcal{L}_{i})({\bar{\nabla}}\mathcal{L}_{i}-\nabla\mathcal{L}_{i})^{T}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]K_{i}^{-1}(I+C_{i})

\displaystyle\quad+\mathbb{E}[\left\{{\bm{z}}_{i,\tau}-(I+C_{i}){\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{i}\right\}\left\{{\bm{z}}_{i,\tau}-(I+C_{i}){\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{i}\right\}^{T}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\eqqcolon\mathcal{J}_{1,i}+\mathcal{J}_{2,i}.

(E.11)

For the term
\mathcal{J}_{1,i}
, we apply Assumption
4.2
and have
\mathbb{E}[({\bar{g}}_{i}-\nabla f_{i})({\bar{g}}_{i}-\nabla f_{i})^{T}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]=\penalty\ \mathbb{E}[{\bar{g}}_{i}{\bar{g}}_{i}^{T}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]-\nabla f_{i}\nabla^{T}f_{i}
. We also note that

\displaystyle\left\|\mathbb{E}[{\bar{g}}_{i}{\bar{g}}_{i}^{T}-\nabla f({\bm{x}}^{\star};\xi)\nabla^{T}f({\bm{x}}^{\star};\xi)\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\right\|

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\left[\|{\bar{g}}_{i}-\nabla f({\bm{x}}^{\star};\xi)\|\cdot\|{\bar{g}}_{i}\|\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]+\mathbb{E}\left[\|{\bar{g}}_{i}-\nabla f({\bm{x}}^{\star};\xi)\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]

\displaystyle\leq 2\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\|{\bar{g}}_{i}-\nabla f({\bm{x}}^{\star};\xi)\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]}\sqrt{\mathbb{E}\left[\|{\bar{g}}_{i}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]}+\mathbb{E}\left[\|{\bar{g}}_{i}-\nabla f({\bm{x}}^{\star};\xi)\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right],

and

\mathbb{E}\left[\|{\bar{g}}_{i}-\nabla f({\bm{x}}^{\star};\xi)\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}\right]\leq\mathbb{E}[\sup_{{\bm{x}}\in\mathcal{X}}\|\nabla^{2}f({\bm{x}};\xi)\|^{2}]\cdot\|{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}^{\star}\|^{2}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\eqref{equ:BM:e}}}{{\leq}}\Upsilon_{m}\|{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}^{\star}\|^{2}.

By Assumptions
4.1
,
4.2
(
4.2a
), we suppose
\|\nabla f_{i}\|\leq\Upsilon_{u}
(we abuse
\Upsilon_{u}
from (
D.2
)) and obtain

\mathbb{E}[\|{\bar{g}}_{i}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]=\|\nabla f_{i}\|^{2}+\mathbb{E}[\|{\bar{g}}_{i}-\nabla f_{i}\|^{2}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\leq\Upsilon_{u}^{2}+\Upsilon_{m}\leq 2(\Upsilon_{u}^{2}\vee\Upsilon_{m}).

Combining the above three displays, we have

\left\|\mathbb{E}[{\bar{g}}_{i}{\bar{g}}_{i}^{T}-\nabla f({\bm{x}}^{\star};\xi)\nabla^{T}f({\bm{x}}^{\star};\xi)\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\right\|\\
\leq 2\sqrt{2\Upsilon_{m}}(\Upsilon_{u}\vee\sqrt{\Upsilon_{m}})(\|{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}^{\star}\|+\|{\bm{x}}_{i}-{\bm{x}}^{\star}\|^{2})\rightarrow 0.

(E.12)

This implies that

\lim\limits_{i\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}[({\bar{g}}_{i}-\nabla f_{i})({\bar{g}}_{i}-\nabla f_{i})^{T}\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]=\mathbb{E}[\nabla f({\bm{x}}^{\star};\xi)\nabla^{T}f({\bm{x}}^{\star};\xi)]-\nabla f({\bm{x}}^{\star})\nabla^{T}f({\bm{x}}^{\star}).

(E.13)

Furthermore, by Lemma
E.4
we know
K_{i}\rightarrow K^{\star}
as
i\rightarrow\infty
. Since
\|K_{i}S(S^{T}K_{t}^{2}S)^{\dagger}S^{T}K_{i}\|\leq 1
, we apply dominated convergence theorem
(
Durrett 2019
, Theorem 1.6.7)
and Lemma
5.2
, and have
\lim_{i\rightarrow\infty}\mathbb{E}[K_{i}S(S^{T}K_{i}^{2}S)^{\dagger}S^{T}K_{i}\mid{\bm{x}}_{i},{\bm{\lambda}}_{i}]=\mathbb{E}[K^{\star}S(S^{T}(K^{\star})^{2}S)^{\dagger}S^{T}K^{\star}]
. Here, the expectation is taken over randomness of
S
. Thus,
C_{i}\rightarrow C^{\star}
. By the definition (
5.5
), we obtain

\mathcal{J}_{1,i}=(I+C^{\star}){\Omega^{\star}}(I+C^{\star})+O(\mathcal{K}_{1,i})

(E.14)

with
\mathcal{K}_{1,i}\rightarrow 0
as
i\rightarrow\infty
almost surely. For the term
\mathcal{J}_{2,i}
, we apply (
D.1
) and define
\widetilde{C}_{i}\coloneqq-\prod_{j=0}^{\tau-1}C_{i,j}
. Then, we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{J}_{2,i}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(\widetilde{C}_{i}-C_{i}){\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{i}{\widetilde{\bm{z}}}_{i}^{T}(\widetilde{C}_{i}^{T}-C_{i}^{T})\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\mathclap{\eqref{equ:Newton}}}}{{=}}\mathbb{E}[(\widetilde{C}_{i}-C_{i})K_{i}^{-1}{\bar{\nabla}}\mathcal{L}_{i}{\bar{\nabla}}^{T}\mathcal{L}_{i}K_{i}^{-1}(\widetilde{C}_{i}^{T}-C_{i}^{T})\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[(\widetilde{C}_{i}-C_{i})K_{i}^{-1}({\bar{\nabla}}\mathcal{L}_{i}-\nabla\mathcal{L}_{i})({\bar{\nabla}}\mathcal{L}_{i}-\nabla\mathcal{L}_{i})^{T}K_{i}^{-1}(\widetilde{C}_{i}^{T}-C_{i}^{T})\mid\mathcal{F}_{i-1}]
